A common cause of brain damage, especially in older people, is temporary interruption of normal blood flow to a brain area during a stroke, also known as a cerebrovascular accident.","In ischemia, the neurons deprived of blood lose much of their oxygen and glucose supplies.","Also, cerebrospinal fluid flows into the brain and rapidly accumulates, causing swelling of the brain that produces widespread damage.","In hemorrhage, neurons are flooded with blood and excess oxygen, calcium, and other chemicals, again causing edema (accumulation of fluid). Both ischemia and hemorrhage also impair the sodium–potassium pump, leading to an accumulation of sodium inside neurons.","The combination of edema and excess sodium releases excess glutamate, which overstimulates neurons, causing damage. Emergency wards and ambulances have improved their response times, but most stroke victims don’t get help quickly enough, because neither they nor their family recognize the problem at once.","However, the mean age of the marijuana users in that study was 47, and the mean for nonusers was 69.","Ischemia kills a cluster of cells that were starved of blood.","Surrounding that cluster is the penumbra, a set of cells that are impaired by having less than the usual amount of blood.","Axons near the damage are also impaired because of chemicals spreading from the area of damage.","More distant cells may also be less active because of decreased input from the killed or impaired cells. Nevertheless, during the weeks after a stroke or other damage, neurons show greater than their usual level of plasticity, comparable to the changes in early brain development.","Let’s consider some of the changes that happen spontaneously and some possibilities for medical intervention.","After damage to any brain area, other areas that have lost part of their normal input become less active.","For example, shortly after damage in one brain hemisphere, its input to the other hemisphere declines, and therefore the other hemisphere shows deficits also.","Diaschisis (di-AS-ki-sis, from a Greek term meaning “to shock throughout”) refers to the decreased activity of surviving neurons because of damage to other neurons.","When diaschisis contributes to behavioral deficits following brain damage, increased stimulation should help. Feeney and colleagues measured the behavioral effects of cortical damage in rats and cats.","Injecting amphetamine significantly enhanced both behaviors, and animals that practiced the behaviors under the influence of amphetamine showed long-lasting benefits.","Damage to the brain or spinal cord kills cells, and it also cuts axons.","Getting the axons to grow back and connect to the correct targets could offer a great benefit.","Damaged axons also grow back in both the retina and the spinal cord of fish, under the control of chemicals that are absent in adult mammals. However, axons do not grow back in the adult mammalian brain or spinal cord, at least not enough to produce any benefit.","A cut in the nervous system causes astrocytes to form scar tissue, thicker in mammals than in fish.","However, later studies indicated that the scar tissue produces a mixture of effects.","Several chemicals can stimulate axon regrowth, and research with laboratory rats has shown that such chemicals sometimes enable axons to return to their normal targets. In each case, to get proper function, the animal needs to practice the relevant movements.","Ordinarily, the surface of dendrites and cell bodies is covered with synapses, and a vacant spot doesn’t stay vacant for long.","After a cell loses input from an axon, it secretes neurotrophins that induce other axons to form new branches, or collateral sprouts, that take over the vacant synapses (Figure 4.23).","In the area near the damage, sprouting and new synapses form at a higher than usual rate, especially in the first weeks. However, the result depends on whether the sprouting axons convey information similar to that of the axons that they replace.","However, if the entorhinal cortex is damaged in both hemispheres, then axons from other locations sprout into the vacant synapses, conveying different information.","Neurons make adjustments to maintain a nearly constant level of arousal.","After learning strengthens one set of synapses, other synapses weaken. (If this didn’t happen, then every time you learned something, your brain would get more and more aroused.) Conversely, if a certain set of synapses becomes inactive—perhaps because of damage to incoming axons—the remaining synapses become more responsive, more easily stimulated.","response, known as denervation supersensitivity or receptor supersensitivity, has been demonstrated mostly with dopamine synapses. However, when either collateral sprouting or denervation supersensitivity occurs, it can strengthen not only the desirable connections but also undesirable ones, sometimes those responsible for pain.","Unfortunately, many treatments that facilitate the regrowth of axons in a damaged spinal cord also lead to chronic pain.","If a brain area loses some of its incoming axons, we can expect denervation supersensitivity, collateral sprouting, or both.","The result is either increased response to a synapse, or response to an axon that previously did not attach at all.","For many years, neuroscientists assumed that the cortical area corresponding to that arm would remain permanently silent, because axons from other cortical areas could not sprout far enough to reach the area representing the arm.","Investigators recorded from the cerebral cortices of monkeys whose sensory nerves from one forelimb had been cut 12 years previously. The change had not required 12 years, but the researchers simply had not checked before then.","Evidently the brain areas that start off as arm areas, hand areas, or whatever retain those properties even after decades without normal input.","Physicians have long noted that many people with amputations experience a phantom limb, a continuing sensation of an amputated body part. However, removing more of the limb in an attempt to eliminate the phantom sensations accomplished nothing.","A touch on the face now produces a facial sensation but it also produces a phantom hand sensation.","Similarly, someone with brain damage may have lost an ability, or may be able to find it with enough effort.","Much recovery from brain damage depends on learning to make better use of","For example, if you lose your peripheral vision, you could move your head from side to side to compensate.","If you lose control of your fingers, you might move your arm and trunk to guide the fingers to a desired place. Often, a person or animal with brain damage who appears unable to do something is just not trying.","Consider an animal that incurred damage to the sensory nerves from a forelimb to the spinal cord, as in Figure 4.26.","The animal no longer feels the limb, but the motor nerves still connect to the muscles. We say the limb is deafferented because it lost its afferent (sensory) input.","A monkey with a deafferented limb does not use it for walking, picking up objects, or any other voluntary behaviors.","Because this company is like a brain, it can’t hire a replacement, because that would be like growing a new neuron.","Temporarily, the company fares badly, because it misses that person’s work, and many other people in the company lack the input they need to do their jobs.","Apparently, a monkey fails to use one deafferented forelimb only because walking on three limbs is easier than using an impaired limb.","Therapy for someone with brain damage begins with careful evaluation of a patient’s abilities and disabilities. Keep separate the properties of receptors, the coding of color, and the visual experience constructed by the brain."},{"type":"heading","id":"bp14-m5-1-study-1","text":"Turning light into neural signals"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Vision begins when light reaches photoreceptors in the retina. The eye focuses incoming light onto a layered sheet of neural tissue; the optic nerve then carries processed retinal signals to the brain. The retina is not just a camera sensor: its neurons already compare inputs and begin extracting patterns. Rods are highly useful in low illumination but contribute little to color vision; cones support higher acuity and color discrimination in brighter light. The fovea has dense cones and is important for detailed central vision, while peripheral retina serves other visual demands."},{"type":"heading","id":"bp14-m5-1-study-2","text":"Why there is a blind spot"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Axons from retinal ganglion cells converge at the optic disc to form the optic nerve. That region lacks ordinary photoreceptors, creating a blind spot in each eye. People usually do not notice it because the eyes provide overlapping information and the visual system organizes a coherent scene. The key point is that experience is not a pixel-for-pixel copy of what strikes the retina; perception depends on the organization and interpretation of neural activity."},{"type":"heading","id":"bp14-m5-1-study-3","text":"Explaining color perception"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"The trichromatic theory accounts for color discrimination through three cone classes with different spectral sensitivities. Opponent-process ideas explain why some color combinations function as opposing dimensions and help account for afterimages and other observations. The retinex approach emphasizes comparison of light from different parts of a scene to maintain relatively stable perceived color under changing illumination. These accounts address different levels of processing rather than requiring one to be discarded entirely."},{"type":"heading","id":"bp14-m5-1-study-4","text":"When color vision differs"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Inherited differences in cone pigments or other stages of processing can change color discrimination. The everyday phrase 'color blindness' covers varied patterns rather than a single complete absence of color experience. Distinguish a receptor-level difference from the brain's later comparison and interpretation of signals. In a test answer, describe how light is encoded, how cone responses are compared, and why color appearance depends on context."},{"type":"heading","id":"deep-bp14-m5-1-h01","text":"Principles of Vision"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"We have been using the term vision to mean perception of light. The brain interprets signals produced when reflected or emitted light reaches the retina; visual perception is therefore an active interpretation rather than a direct copy of the outside world.","Light from a distant source or a nearby object must travel to the eye before the visual receptors can respond. Sight is not projected outward from the observer.","Light enters the eye through an opening in the center of the iris called the pupil (Figure 5.1).","It is focused by the lens (adjustable) and cornea (not adjustable) and projected onto the retina, the rear surface of the eye, which is lined with visual","Light from the left side of the world strikes the right half of the retina, and vice versa.","Light from above strikes the bottom half of the retina, and light from below strikes the top half. The inversion of the image poses no problem for the nervous system.","It codes it by various kinds of neuronal activity.","In the vertebrate retina, however, messages go from the receptors at the back of the eye to bipolar cells, located closer to the center of the eye (Figure 5.2).","The bipolar cells send their messages to ganglion cells, located still closer to the center of the eye.","Additional cells called amacrine cells get information from bipolar cells and send it to bipolar cells, ganglion cells, and other amacrine cells. One consequence of this anatomy is that light passes through the ganglion, amacrine, and bipolar cells en route to the receptors.","The point at which it leaves (also where the blood vessels enter and leave) is a blind spot because it has no receptors.","page is about 10 inches (25 cm) away, the x disappears because its image strikes the blind spot. Second, anything in the blind spot of one eye is visible to the other eye.","Because blood vessels and ganglion cell axons are almost absent near the fovea, it has nearly unimpeded vision.","More importantly for perceiving detail, each receptor in the fovea connects to a single bipolar cell, which in turn connects to a single ganglion cell that has an axon to the brain.","The ganglion cells in the fovea of humans and other primates are called midget ganglion cells because each is small and responds to just a single cone. That is, each cone in the fovea has a direct route to the brain.","Because the midget ganglion cells provide 70 percent of the input to the brain, your vision is dominated by what you see in and near the fovea.","That arrangement is adaptive because predatory birds spend most of their day looking down, either while flying or while perched high in a tree.","However, feeding so many receptors into the same bipolar or ganglion cell loses information about the exact shape or location of an object. For the second display, it is harder to read that same letter in the same location, because of interference from the neighboring letters.","The rods, abundant in the periphery of the human retina, respond to faint light but are less useful in daylight because bright light bleaches them.","Cones, abundant in and near the fovea, are more useful in bright light and essential for color vision.","Because of the distribution of rods and cones, humans have good color vision in the fovea but not in the periphery.","Although rods outnumber cones by about 20 to 1 in the human retina, cones provide about 90 percent of the brain’s input. Overall, 120 million rods and 6 million cones converge onto 1 million axons in the optic nerve, on average.","A 20:1 ratio of rods to cones may sound high, but the ratio is even higher in species that are active at night.","Some people have two or three times as many axons from the eyes to the brain as others do. Researchers find that top performers in tennis, squash, fencing, baseball, and badminton demonstrate enhanced processing of visual stimuli, compared to other people.","Both rods and cones contain photopigments, chemicals that release energy when struck by light.","Visible light consists of electromagnetic radiation within the range from less than 400 nm (nanometer, or 10 –9 m) to more than 700 nm.","We call these wavelengths “light” only because the receptors in our eyes are tuned to detecting them.","Indeed, many species of birds, fish, and insects have visual receptors sensitive to what we call ultraviolet radiation. We cannot know what it looks like to them, but so far as they are concerned, ultraviolet radiation is a type of light.","In some species of birds, the male and female look alike to us, but the birds see a difference, because the male reflects more ultraviolet light.","People distinguish red, green, yellow, blue, orange, pink, purple, greenish blue, and so forth.","He proposed that we perceive color by comparing the responses across a few types of receptors, each of which was sensitive to a different range of wavelengths.","According to this theory, we perceive color through the relative rates of response by three Therefore, he concluded that three kinds of receptors—we now call them cones—are sufficient to account for human color vision.","According to the trichromatic theory, humans discriminate among wavelengths by the ratio of activity across the three types of cones.","For example, light at 550 nm excites the medium-wavelength and long-wavelength receptors about equally and the short-wavelength receptor almost not at all. More intense light increases the activity of all three cones without much change in their ratio of responses.","Think about this example of coding: Your perception of color depends on the frequency of response in each cell relative to the frequency of other cells. Because of the haphazard arrangement, moderatesized patches of the retina have only medium- or longwavelength cones.","That is, the brain has a mechanism that perceives color on a continuum from red to green, another from yellow to blue, and another from white to black.","It increases its activity in response to medium-wavelength (green) light and decreases it in response to reddish light. However, the explanation must go beyond the connections from cones to the bipolar cells.","The opponent-process theory explains why colors are experienced in contrasting pairs and why staring at one color can produce an afterimage of its opposite. It complements, rather than simply replaces, the trichromatic account of cone receptors.","Visual processing includes opposing red–green, yellow–blue, and black–white dimensions. A neural response along one direction of a pair limits or counterbalances the opposing direction.","Neither the trichromatic theory nor the opponent-process theory can easily explain color constancy, the ability to recognize colors despite changes in lighting.","The bottom part illustrates the squares that looked blue in the top left part and yellow in the top right part.","Without the context that indicated yellow light or blue light, all these squares look gray. For this reason, we should be cautious about talking about the color of a wavelength of light.","Similarly, we perceive the brightness of an object by comparing it to other objects.","The object in the center appears to have a dark gray top and a white bottom.","Dale Purves and colleagues have expressed a similar idea in more general terms: Whenever we see anything, we make an inference. That is, visual perception is a process of reasoning and inference, not just retinal stimulation.","Then investigators demonstrated that some people have otherwise satisfactory vision without seeing all the color that other people do.","That is, color is in the brain, not in the light or the object itself.","Color vision deficiency results when people with certain genes fail to develop one type of cone, or develop an abnormal type of cone.","In red-green color deficiency, the most common form of color deficiency, people have trouble distinguishing red from green because their long- and medium-wavelength cones have the same photopigment instead of different ones. About 8 percent of Northern European men (and a smaller percentage of men from other backgrounds) are red-green colorblind, compared with less than 1 percent of women.","Women with one normal gene and one color-deficient gene—and that includes all women with a father with red-green color deficiency—have slightly less than average ability to distinguish red from green.","I remember once explaining to my then teenage son a newly discovered detail about the visual system, only to have him reply, “I didn’t realize it would be so complicated.","We have such devices in our brains, and they produce the amazing results that we call vision.","Researchers took adult monkeys with red-green color deficiency and used gene therapy to add a third kind of cone to their retinas.","Because the gene is on the X chromosome, a man has only one or the other. Because women have two X chromosomes, some women have one receptor with serine and one with alanine.","Examining the retina does not help because invertebrate receptors resemble neither rods nor cones.","Kalat uses the history of research on sight to introduce this distinction. To understand how vision works, first separate the physical journey of light from the neural processes that turn it into experience.","Light affects receptors in the retina, and their activity changes signals sent toward the brain. The brain must work with those signals rather than with the object directly.","Seeing involves the processing of visual signals. What appears to be a simple visual picture depends on the receptors, connections, and brain mechanisms described in the rest of the module.","The same physical light can be processed differently depending on retinal position and neural context. This sets up later topics on color, visual fields, and perception.","Kalat presents negative color afterimages as the central observation: after prolonged viewing of one color, the opposing response becomes relatively stronger when a neutral surface is viewed.","After sustained exposure to red, a neutral background may appear green; sustained yellow can be followed by a blue afterimage. This effect is difficult to explain from cone types alone.","Connections among retinal cells combine excitatory and inhibitory input from different cone classes. Their balance helps create responses to color contrasts.","Opponent coding clarifies afterimages, but color constancy and contextual color effects require additional explanation, developed in the next section on retinex Brain","kind":"detailed_source_grounded_notes","printedStart":161,"printedEnd":175,"pdfStart":179,"pdfEnd":193,"content":[{"type":"paragraph","text":"Visual signals are transformed at several stages between the retina and cerebral cortex. Retinal processing, receptive fields, and cortical responses help explain how the brain extracts useful visual features."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"The development of visual cortex also depends on early experience. Read the deprivation findings as evidence about sensitive periods and neural wiring rather than as a claim that sight is a passive recording of the world."},{"type":"heading","id":"bp14-m5-2-study-1","text":"The route from eye to cortex"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Visual information leaves the retina through retinal ganglion cell axons and travels through central pathways that include the lateral geniculate nucleus of the thalamus and primary visual cortex. Signals from the two eyes are organized so the brain can combine information about space while preserving important distinctions. Processing already occurs in retinal circuits and continues through interconnected cortical regions. A complete explanation needs both the pathway and the transformations performed along it."},{"type":"heading","id":"bp14-m5-2-study-2","text":"Receptive fields and edge processing"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"A receptive field is the region of sensory space in which stimulation affects a neuron's activity. Retinal and thalamic cells often respond to patterns such as differences between center and surrounding light, allowing the system to emphasize contrast. In visual cortex, some cells respond best to oriented edges or combinations of features. The 'feature detector' label is useful but incomplete: responses depend on context, networks, and past experience. Recognition cannot be explained by stacking isolated detectors without understanding their interactions."},{"type":"heading","id":"bp14-m5-2-study-3","text":"How experience shapes visual cortex"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Early visual experience helps organize cortical circuits. Restricted input to one eye, both eyes, or particular patterns can alter later responses; the outcome depends on the age and duration of the deprivation. Binocular cooperation depends partly on correlated visual stimulation. These findings are important for understanding why some visual problems in infancy need attention early, while also showing that visual development involves more than genetically fixed wiring."},{"type":"heading","id":"bp14-m5-2-study-4","text":"From local responses to perception"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Seeing an object requires processing contours, color, motion, and spatial relations across many neuronal populations. The visual cortex has specialized response properties, yet its function rests on a network of feedforward and feedback connections. A diagram of the pathway tells you where information travels; receptive-field experiments tell you what particular populations respond to.
